\alpha_{i}=1-\varepsilon+\beta_{i}^{(\varepsilon)}(\log(1/\varepsilon))^{-2}\varepsilon,\text{ where
}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\beta^{(\varepsilon)}_{i}=\beta_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}},\ \ i=0,1,

so
that our voter model perturbation rates are given by

\displaystyle c_{\varepsilon}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=c^{{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+\varepsilon g_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}})\widehat{\xi}(x)+\varepsilon g_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}})\xi(x),

\displaystyle g_{i}^{\varepsilon}(\xi|_{\mathcal{N}})

\displaystyle=\big(-1+(\log 1/\varepsilon)^{-2}\beta_{1-i}^{(\varepsilon)}\big)f_{i}(0,\xi)^{2},\ \ i=0,1.

If
g_{i}(\xi|_{\mathcal{N}})=-f_{i}(0,\xi)^{2}
, clearly

\|g_{i}^{\varepsilon}-g_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq C(\log(1/\varepsilon))^{-2}\to 0\ \ \text{ for }i=0,1.

Obviously both
{\bf 0}
and
{\bf 1}
are traps
for each
\varepsilon
, and we also have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}(\log(1/\varepsilon))^{2}(g_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\xi)-g_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\widehat{\xi}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}(\beta_{0}^{(\varepsilon)}-\beta_{1}^{(\varepsilon)})f_{1}(0,\xi)^{2}

\displaystyle=(\beta_{0}-\beta_{1})f_{1}(0,\xi)^{2}

\displaystyle:=g^{a}(\xi).

Therefore
\{c_{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
is an asymptotically
symmetric finite range voter model perturbation. We have

\beta_{i}^{N}:=\Bigl(\frac{\log N}{\log(1/\varepsilon_{N})}\Bigr)^{2}\beta^{(\varepsilon_{N})}_{i}\to\beta_{i}\text{ as
}N\to\infty,

and so, for
\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}
,

\displaystyle r^{N,s}(A)=g_{0}^{\varepsilon_{N}}(1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A})

\displaystyle=(-1+(\log N)^{-2}\beta_{1}^{N})\Bigl(\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}}\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\to-\Bigl(\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}|}\Bigr)^{2}=r^{s}(A),

(3.44)

and

\displaystyle r^{N,a}(A)

\displaystyle=(\log N)^{2}[g^{\varepsilon_{N}}_{1}(1_{A})-g^{\varepsilon_{N}}_{0}(1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A})]

\displaystyle=(\beta^{N}_{0}-\beta^{N}_{1})f_{1}(0,1_{A})^{2}

\displaystyle\to(\beta_{0}-\beta_{1})\Bigl(\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}|}\Bigr)^{2}=r^{a}(A).

Therefore in this case we have

\Theta_{2}=\Theta_{2}^{\texttt{lv}}:=(\beta_{0}-\beta_{1})\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}\Big(\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}|}\Big)^{2}K_{2}(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A),

(3.45)

and

\Theta_{3}=\Theta_{3}^{\texttt{lv}}:=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}\Big(\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}|}\Big)^{2}(\Theta^{-}(A)-\Theta^{+}(A)).

(3.46)

Example 3.10
.

(Affine Voter Models) The 2-dimensional threshold voter model rate
function, introduced in
[
5
]
, and corresponding to
q=0
in
Example
3.8
, is

c^{\texttt{tv}}(x,\xi)=1\{\xi(x+y)\neq\xi(x)\text{ for some }y\in{\mathcal{N}}\}

for a neighbourhood
{\mathcal{N}}
. The affine voter model with parameter

\alpha\in[0,1]
, introduced in
[
31
]
, is the spin-flip system
with rate function

c^{\texttt{av}}(x,\xi)=\alpha c^{{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+(1-\alpha)c^{{\texttt{tv}}}(x,\xi).

Here the voter model kernel is
p(x)=1_{\mathcal{N}}(x)/|{\mathcal{N}}|
, and

0
and
1
are traps. The fact that
c^{\texttt{tv}}
is
cancellative was noted in Section 2
of
[
5
]
. It is easy to check that a convex
combination of cancellative rate functions is cancellative,
and hence
c^{\texttt{av}}
is cancellative, in fact good cancellative for

\alpha<1
.
Monotonicity of
c^{\texttt{tv}}
, and hence of
c^{\texttt{av}}
is clear.
Letting
\varepsilon=1-\alpha
,

c^{\texttt{av}}(x,\xi)=c^{\texttt{vm}}(x,\xi)+\varepsilon(c^{{\texttt{tv}}}(x,\xi)-c^{\texttt{vm}}(x,\xi))

so that our voter model perturbation rates are given by

\displaystyle c_{\varepsilon}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=c^{{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+\varepsilon\big(\widehat{\xi}(x)g_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}})+\xi(x)g_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}})\big),

\displaystyle g_{i}^{\varepsilon}(\xi|_{\mathcal{N}})

\displaystyle=-f_{i}(0,\xi)+1\{n_{i}(0,\xi)\geq 1\}\ \ i=0,1.

Since
g_{i}=g_{i}^{\varepsilon}
does not depend on
\varepsilon
,
(
1.15
) holds.
Obviously each
c_{\varepsilon}
is symmetric,
so
r^{N,a}=r^{a}=g^{a}\equiv 0
. Therefore
\{c_{\varepsilon}:\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
is
an asymptotically symmetric finite range voter model
perturbation such that for
A\neq\emptyset
,

\displaystyle r^{N,s}(A)=r^{s}(A)=g_{0}(1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A})=-\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}|}+1,

\Theta_{2}=0
, and if we use (
3.34
) and then
(
3.35
), we get

\Theta_{3}=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}\Big(1-\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}|}\Big)(\Theta^{+}(A)-\Theta^{-}(A))=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}(\Theta^{+}(A)-\Theta^{-}(A))=\kappa\ :=\Theta^{{\texttt{av}}}_{3}.

(3.47)

Example 3.11
.

(Geometric Voter Models) The 2-dimensional
geometric voter model with rate function, introduced in
[
5
]
,
is

c^{\texttt{gv}}(x,\xi)=\dfrac{1-\theta^{j}}{1-\theta^{|{\mathcal{N}}|}}\text{ if }\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{N}}}1\{\xi(x+y)\neq\xi(x)\}=j.

(3.48)

Here
{\mathcal{N}}
is a neighbourhood,
0\leq\theta<1
, and

0
and
1
are clearly traps. As
\theta
ranges from 0 to 1,
these dynamics range from the threshold voter model to the voter
model. The fact that
c^{\texttt{gv}}
is cancellative was proved in Section 2
of
[
5
]
, and it follows that
c^{\texttt{gv}}
is then good
cancellative. Monotonicity of
c^{\texttt{gv}}
is again elementary. By (7.3)
in
[
13
]
, taking
\varepsilon=1-\theta
(instead of
\varepsilon^{2}
),

c^{\texttt{gv}}(x,\xi)=c^{\texttt{vm}}(x,\xi)+\varepsilon\frac{|{\mathcal{N}}|}{2}f_{0}(x,\xi)f_{1}(x,\xi)+O(\varepsilon^{2})\text{ as }\varepsilon\to 0.

(3.49)

Here,
c^{\texttt{vm}}
and the densities
f_{i}
use the kernel

p(x)=1_{{\mathcal{N}}}(x)/|{\mathcal{N}}|
, and the
O(\varepsilon^{2})
term is uniform in

x,\xi
. Thus, there are
g^{\varepsilon}_{i}
such that

\displaystyle c_{\varepsilon}(x,\xi)=c^{{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+\varepsilon g_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}})\widehat{\xi}(x)+\varepsilon g_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}})\xi(x),

where if
g(x,\xi)=(|{\mathcal{N}}|/2)f_{0}(x,\xi)f_{1}(x,\xi)
, then
\|g_{i}^{\varepsilon}-g\|\leq C|\varepsilon|
. Clearly
c^{{\texttt{gv}}}
is symmetric and so by
Remark
3.6

g^{a}=r^{a}=0
. Therefore
\{c_{\varepsilon},0\leq\varepsilon<1\}

is an asymptotically symmetric voter model perturbation such that for

A\neq\emptyset
,

r^{s}(A)=g(1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A})=\frac{|A|(|{\mathcal{N}}|-|A|)}{2|{\mathcal{N}}|}.

Thus
\Theta_{2}=0
and, again using (
3.34
), we have

\displaystyle\Theta_{3}=\frac{1}{2|{\mathcal{N}}|}\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}|A|(|{\mathcal{N}}|-|A|)(\Theta^{+}(A)-\Theta^{-}(A))

\displaystyle=\frac{|{\mathcal{N}}|}{2}\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}\Bigl(\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}|}\Bigr)^{2}(\Theta^{-}(A)-\Theta^{+}(A))

(3.50)

\displaystyle:=\Theta^{\texttt{gv}}_{3}=\frac{|{\mathcal{N}}|}{2}\Theta_{3}^{\texttt{lv}}.

3.3
Proof of Theorem
1.1
for dimensions 3
and above

We assume
{\mathcal{N}}
is as in Theorem
1.1
for
d\geq 3
. Let

\langle\quad\rangle_{u}
denote expectation with respect to the voter
model equilibrium with density
u\in[0,1]
and define symmetric rates

f(u)=\langle(1-\xi(0))c^{*}(0,\xi)-\xi(0)c^{*}(0,\xi)\rangle_{u}\text{ for }u\in[0,1],

(3.51)

where
c^{*}
is as in (
3.38
) but with
r_{\ell}
in
place of
r_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}
. As noted in Section 1 of
[
7
]

f

will be a polynomial in
u
of degree at most
|{\mathcal{N}}|+1
. As we have

d\geq 3
, Theorem 1.2 of
[
13
]
(as strengthened in
Remark
1.10
) shows that the conclusion of
Theorem
1.1
will hold for
q<1
and sufficiently close to
1
,
providing the following hold for some
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
:

\displaystyle(1)\text{ For $0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}$, $c^{(1-\varepsilon)}(x,\xi)$
is a rate function of a cancellative process (as in \eqref{canc}).}

\displaystyle(2)\ \{c^{(1-\varepsilon)}(x,\xi):0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}\}\text{ is a finite range
voter model perturbation.}

\displaystyle(3)\ f^{\prime}(0)>0.

Here we have ignored one condition from Theorem 1.2 of

[
13
]
(condition (1.2) there) as the required exponential tail
bound is trivially true for our finite neighbourhood setting. The
first two conditions have been established in Sections
3.1

(Lemma
3.3
) and
3.2
, respectively. Finally (3)
follows from Theorem 1.2 of
[
4
]
, where it is shown for any
neighbourhood
{\mathcal{N}}
. ∎

Remark 3.12
.

The proof of
f^{\prime}(0)>0
from
[
4
]

is stated for
d=3
but holds equally well for
d\geq 3
. In fact it
gives a stronger representation for
f
which implies
f^{\prime}(0)>0
.
Alternatively see Proposition
5.9
for a more direct
proof.

4
A general complete convergence theorem in two dimensions

Fix an initial state
\xi_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
. For

\xi,\eta\in\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
. We first extend the stochastic
differential equation (SDE) construction of spin-flip systems and
coupling with killed processes from Section 2 of
[
11
]
to the
setting where
|\xi_{0}|
may be infinite. Let
\{N^{x,i}:x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2},i=0,1\}
be independent Poisson point processes on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
with rate

ds\times du
. For
R\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
T\geq 0
,

{\mathcal{G}}([0,T]\times R)\text{ is the
$\sigma$-field generated by }\{N^{x,i}|_{[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}}:x\in R,i=0,1\}.

(4.1)

Consider a rate function
c:{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\times\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\to[0,\infty)
,
which is bounded continuous. Let
\hat{c}(x,\xi)=c(x,\widehat{\xi})
be the rate
function for the evolution of the
0
’s. We assume

\sum_{x}\big(c(x,\xi)+\hat{c}(x,\xi)\big)\leq C|\xi|,

(4.2)

and

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\sum_{u\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\sup_{\xi}|c(x,\xi)-c(x,\xi^{(u)})|\leq C,

(4.3)

where

\xi^{(u)}(x)=1(x\neq u)\xi(x)+1(x=u)\widehat{\xi}(x).

Under (
4.3
) and boundedness and continuity of

c
, there is a unique Feller process
\xi_{t}
taking values in

\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
associated with the rate function
c
(see Theorem B3
in
[
25
]
). Note that the above conditions hold for
c
iff they
hold for
\hat{c}
.

Remark 4.1
.

It is easy to check all of the above
conditions are satisfied by
c_{\varepsilon}
, if
\{c_{\varepsilon}:0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\}

are the rates of a finite range voter model perturbation. See
Corollary 2.4 of
[
11
]
for (
4.2
) (without the

\hat{c}
term) and (
4.3
). Condition
(
4.2
) follows easily for
\hat{c}
from the fact
that
{\bf 1}
is a trap for our finite range voter model
perturbations in
d=2
(just as it followed for
c
using the fact
that
{\bf 0}
is a trap). Note that under our boundedness assumption
on
c
, (
4.2
) for
c
alone is equivalent to
condition (2.1) in
[
11
]
.

For
\xi_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
consider the SDE

\xi_{t}(x)=\xi_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int(1-\xi_{s-}(x))1(u\leq c(x,\xi_{s-}))N^{x,0}(ds,du)\\
-\int_{0}^{t}\int\xi_{s-}(x)1(u\leq c(x,\xi_{s-}))N^{x,1}(ds,du),\quad t\geq 0,x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}.

(4.4)

If
|\xi_{0}|<\infty
, (
4.4
) has a pathwise unique solution

\xi_{t}=\xi_{t}[\xi_{0}]
which has the same law as the above Feller
process with initial condition
\xi_{0}
. For this see Proposition
2.1(a) of
[
11
]
and note, that monotonicity of
c
is not needed
in Proposition 2.1(c). Now fix
M_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, set
I^{\prime}=(-M_{0},M_{0})^{2}
, and
let
\underline{c}(x,\xi)=1(x\in I^{\prime})c(x,\xi)
. Clearly
\underline{c}

also satisfies all the hypotheses we have imposed on
c
. For our
given initial condition
\xi_{0}
, let
\underline{\xi}_{0}(x)=1(x\in I^{\prime})\xi_{0}(x)
. Then
|\underline{\xi}_{0}|<\infty
(even if
|\xi_{0}|=\infty
) and so
there is a unique solution,
\underline{\xi}_{t}=\underline{\xi}_{t}[\underline{\xi}_{0},I^{\prime}]
, of

\underline{\xi}_{t}(x)=\underline{\xi}_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int(1-\underline{\xi}_{s-}(x))1(u\leq\underline{c}(x,\underline{\xi}_{s-}))N^{x,0}(ds,du)\\
-\int_{0}^{t}\int\underline{\xi}_{s-}(x)1(u\leq\underline{c}(x,\underline{\xi}_{s-}))N^{x,1}(ds,du),\quad t\geq 0,x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}.

(4.5)

Note that
\underline{\xi}_{t}(x)=0
for all
t\geq 0
and all
x\notin I^{\prime}
. If, in
addition,
c
is monotone, then by Proposition 2.1(b) of
[
11
]
,

\text{if $\xi_{\cdot}$ satisfies
}(\ref{construct2})\text{ where $|\xi_{0}|<\infty$, then
}\underline{\xi}_{t}\leq\xi_{t}\ \forall t\geq 0.

(4.6)

We now show this continues to hold even if
|\xi_{0}|=\infty
.
Unless otherwise indicated, monotonicity of the rate function,
c
, is
assumed in the rest of this section.
Note that
\hat{c}
is monotone
iff
c
is. Therefore our hypotheses hold for
c
iff they hold for

\hat{c}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

If
\xi_{\cdot}

satisfies (
4.4
) then
\underline{\xi}_{t}\leq\xi_{t}
for all
t\geq 0

a.s.

Proof.

By (
4.6
), we may focus on

|\xi_{0}|=\infty
. Define

\Lambda_{t}=\sum_{x\in I^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\|c\|_{\infty}}(N^{x,0}+N^{x,1})(ds,du).

Then
\Lambda
is a Poisson
process with rate
2|I^{\prime}|\|c\|_{\infty}<\infty
and so has a
sequence of jump times
0<T_{1}<T_{2}<\dots
increasing to infinity. It
suffices to prove that
\underline{\xi}_{t}\leq\xi_{t}
for
t\in[0,T_{n}]
for all

n
, which we prove by induction. Assume the inequality up to, and
including, time
T_{n}
. On
(T_{n},T_{n+1})
,

\underline{\xi}_{t}(x)=\underline{\xi}_{T_{n}}(x)\leq\xi_{T_{n}}(x)=\xi_{t}(x)
for all
x\in I^{\prime}

since the jump times of these coordinates are clearly included in the
jump times of
\Lambda
. For
x\notin I^{\prime}
,
\underline{\xi}_{t}(x)=0\leq\xi_{t}(x)

for all
t
, including those in
(T_{n},T_{n+1})
. At
T_{n+1}
one
considers the unique
x\in I^{\prime}
for which
N^{x,i}
jumps at
T_{n+1}

for some
i
and uses the monotonicity of
c
to show that in each of
the two cases
\Delta\xi_{T_{n+1}}(x)<0
or

\Delta\underline{\xi}_{T_{n+1}}(x)>0
, one has

\underline{\xi}_{T_{n+1}}(x)\leq\xi_{T_{n+1}}(x)
. This is done just as in the
proof of Proposition 2.1(b) in
[
11
]
. The remaining cases
trivially lead to the same conclusion. This establishes the induction
step, and the
n=1
step is handled in exactly the same way.
∎

If
\xi_{\cdot}
satisfies
(
4.4
), then
\widehat{\xi}_{\cdot}
satisfies

\widehat{\xi}_{t}(x)=\widehat{\xi}_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int(1-{\widehat{\xi}}_{s-}(x))1(u\leq\hat{c}(x,\widehat{\xi}_{s-}))N^{x,1}(ds,du)\\
-\int_{0}^{t}\int\widehat{\xi}_{s-}(x)1(u\leq\hat{c}(x,\widehat{\xi}_{s-}))N^{x,0}(ds,du),\quad t\geq 0,x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}.

(4.7)

Note that
c
has been replaced with
\hat{c}
and the roles of

N^{x,0}
and
N^{x,1}
have been reversed from that in
(
4.4
). We set
\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{0}(x)=\widehat{\xi}_{0}(x)1(x\in I^{\prime})

and define
\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{t}=\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{t}[\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{0},I^{\prime}]
as the unique
solution (by Proposition 2.1(a) of
[
11
]
because
\hat{c}

satisfies the same hypotheses as
c
) to

\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{t}(x)=\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int(1-{\hat{}\underline{\xi}}_{s-}(x))1(u\leq\underline{\hat{c}}(x,\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{s-}))N^{x,1}(ds,du)\\
-\int_{0}^{t}\int\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{s-}(x)1(u\leq\underline{\hat{c}}(x,\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{s-}))N^{x,0}(ds,du),\quad t\geq 0,x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}.

(4.8)

So in the notation
\hat{}\underline{\xi}
we effectively take the hat first and then do the killing.

If
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\xi_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
, we may set

\underline{\xi}_{0}(x)=\xi^{M}_{0}(x):=\xi_{0}(x)1(x\in(-M,M)^{2})
, and denote the unique
solution to (
4.4
) with this initial state by
\xi^{M}_{t}
.

Proposition 4.3
.

(a) As
M\to\infty
,

\xi^{M}_{t}(x)\uparrow\xi^{\infty}_{t}(x)
for all
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
and
t\geq 0

a.s. Moreover
\xi^{\infty}_{t}
is the unique in law Feller process with
rates
c(x,\xi)
starting at
\xi_{0}
. If
|\xi_{0}|<\infty
then

\xi^{\infty}_{t}=\xi_{t}[\xi_{0}]
, the unique solution of(
4.4
).

(b) We have
\underline{\xi}_{t}(x)\leq\xi^{\infty}_{t}(x)
, and

\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{t}(x)\leq\widehat{\xi^{\infty}_{t}}(x)
for all
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}

t\geq 0
a.s.

(c) If
c
is symmetric, then
P(\hat{}\underline{\xi}\in\cdot)=P_{\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{0}}(\underline{\xi}\in\cdot)
, where the right-hand side is the
law of
\underline{\xi}_{\cdot}
with initial state
\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{0}
, that is, the law
of
{\underline{\xi}_{\cdot}}[\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{0},I^{\prime}]
.

Proof.

(a) By Proposition 2.1(b) of
[
11
]
,
\xi^{M}_{t}(x)

increases in
M
for all
(t,x)
a.s. and so we can define

\xi^{\infty}
as this a.s. limit. By (c) of the same Proposition the
law of
\xi^{M}
is that of the unique Feller process with rates
c
and
initial condition
\xi_{0}^{M}
. Theorem B3 of
[
25
]
allows us to
apply Theorem 5.2 of
[
24
]
to conclude that the martingale
problem associated with the rates
c
is well-posed, and then
Proposition 6.5 of
[
24
]
gives continuity of the laws in the
initial condition. This implies that
\xi^{\infty}
has the required
law. If
|\xi_{0}|<\infty
and
\xi_{t}=\xi_{t}[\xi_{0}]
is the unique
solution of (
4.4
), then monotonicity in the initial
condition from Proposition 2.1(b) of
[
11
]
shows that

\xi^{M}_{t}\leq\xi_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
a.s., and so taking limits we get

\xi^{\infty}_{t}\leq\xi_{t}
for all
t\geq 0
a.s. Since
\xi_{\cdot}
and

\xi_{\cdot}^{\infty}
have the same law, they must be identical.

(b) Let
M\geq M_{0}
, so that

\underline{\xi}^{M}_{0}:=\xi^{M}_{0}(x)1(x\in I^{\prime})=\underline{\xi}_{0}(x)
and therefore

\underline{\xi}_{t}=\underline{\xi}^{M}_{t}:=\underline{\xi}_{t}(\underline{\xi}^{M}_{0},I^{\prime})
. By (
4.6
),
w.p.
1
for any
t\geq 0
,

\underline{\xi}_{t}=\underline{\xi}^{M}_{t}\leq\xi^{M}_{t}\leq\xi^{\infty}_{t}.

If

\hat{}\underline{\xi}^{M}_{t}=\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{t}(\hat{\xi}^{M}_{0},I^{\prime})
, then, just as above with

\hat{c}
in place of
c
,
\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{t}=\hat{}\underline{\xi}^{M}_{t}
. The process

\widehat{\xi}^{M}_{t}=1-\xi^{M}_{t}
satisfies (
4.7
), and so we may
apply Lemma
4.2
with
\hat{c}
in place of
c
and
the roles of
N^{x,0}
and
N^{x,1}
reversed and so conclude that

\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{t}=\hat{}\underline{\xi}^{M}_{t}\leq 1-\xi^{M}_{t}\to\widehat{\xi_{t}^{\infty}}\text{
as }M\to\infty\quad\forall t\geq 0\ \text{a.s.},

and so deduce the
second inequality in (b).

(c) Under symmetry of
c
,
\hat{}\underline{\xi}
is the unique solution
of (
4.8
) with
\hat{c}=c
, and so has the same law
as
\underline{\xi}[\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{0},I^{\prime}]
, the unique solution of
(
4.5
) with initial condition
\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{0}
, because

(N^{x,0},N^{x,1})
is equal in law to
(N^{x,1},N^{x,0})
.
∎

In view of (a) of the above we will denote
\xi_{t}^{\infty}
by

\xi_{t}[\xi_{0}]
as it agrees with our earlier definition for

|\xi_{0}|<\infty
. The reader will note however, we have side-stepped
the general question of pathwise existence and uniqueness of solutions
to (
4.4
) when
|\xi_{0}|=\infty
. We believe this to be the
case by uniqueness of the martingale problem and monotonicity, but
will not need it.

(a) and (b) show that for any initial state
\xi_{0}
, and corresponding

\underline{\xi}_{0}
and
\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{0}
, we may construct
(\xi,\underline{\xi},\hat{}\underline{\xi})
on
the same space such that

\underline{\xi}_{t}\leq\xi_{t}\text{ and }\hat{}\underline{\xi}_{t}\leq\widehat{\xi}(t)\ \ \forall t\geq 0\ a.s.

(4.9)

Here
\underline{\xi}
evolves according to rate
c
with killing
outside
I^{\prime}
, and
\hat{}\underline{\xi}
evolves according to rate
\hat{c}
with
the same killing, while
\xi
and
\widehat{\xi}
evolve according the rates

c
and
\hat{c}
, respectively with no killing. Under symmetry, the
dynamics of the two killed processes are the same but of course the
initial conditions differ. In the symmetric case this point seems to
be made implicitly in the proof of Theorem 1.1 in Section 6 of

[
13
]
, but perhaps warrants the explicit construction given
above.

Turning to the complete convergence theorem, we first present an
abstract complete convergence in two dimensions essentially taken from

[
13
]
. If
A\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
,
x_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
and

\xi\in\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
, let

A(x_{0},\xi)=\{y\in A:\xi(y)=1,\xi(y+x_{0})=0\}.

Monotonicity is not
required for our first abstract complete convergence theorem.

Theorem 4.4
.

Assume for
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
is a cancellative finite
range voter model perturbation with rate function
c_{\varepsilon}(x,\xi)
.
Assume also that for each
\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\exists x_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{ so that if
}|\widehat{\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}}|=\infty\text{ then }\lim_{K\to\infty}\sup_{A\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2},|A|\geq K}\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}}(|\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}|>0,\ A(x_{0},\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{t})=\emptyset)=0,

(4.10)

and

\limsup_{t\to\infty}P_{\delta_{0}}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}(0)=1)>0.

(4.11)

There is an
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
, there is a
translation invariant symmetric stationary distribution

{\nu}^{\varepsilon}_{1/2}
with density
1/2
, satisfying the coexistence
property, such that for all initial
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}
,

\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}\Rightarrow\beta_{0}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0})\delta_{\mbox{\bf 0}}+\beta_{\infty}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}){\nu}^{\varepsilon}_{1/2}+\beta_{1}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0})\delta_{\mbox{\bf 1}}\quad\text{
as }t\to\infty.

(4.12)

Proof.

We first show that for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
is
a good cancellative process (as defined in
Section
3.1
). Assume not. By Remark
3.1
, up
to a constant time change,
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
is a voter model with kernel

q_{0}(y)=\beta_{0}(\{y\})
. Therefore starting at

\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}=\delta_{0}
,
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}=\mbox{\bf 0}
for
t
large
a.s. (e.g. see Proposition V.4.1(b) of
[
24
]
), and this
contradicts (
4.11
), completing the proof.

Under the above hypotheses, Remark 4, Corollary 3.3, and Lemma 4.2 of

[
13
]
show that for some
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, the hypotheses of
Proposition 4.1 of
[
13
]
hold for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
. (Note
that the
good
cancellative property is needed to apply
Corollary 3.3.) That Proposition implies the stationary measure

\nu^{\varepsilon}_{1/2}
(from Section
3.1
) satisfies the
coexistence property and

\text{ if
}|\widehat{\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}}|=\infty,\text{ then
}\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}\Rightarrow\beta_{0}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0})\delta_{\mbox{\bf 0}}+\beta_{\infty}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}){\nu}^{\varepsilon}_{1/2}+\beta_{1}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0})\delta_{\mbox{\bf 1}}\text{
as }t\to\infty,

(4.13)

where we have also used (
1.3
). The fact that
0
is a
trap for
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
(by (
1.16
)) implies
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}

is symmetric by the equivalence of (
3.3
) and (
3.4
)
noted in Section
3.1
. Therefore
\nu^{\varepsilon}_{1/2}
is also
a symmetric law (
\nu^{\varepsilon}_{1/2}(\widehat{\xi}\in\cdot)=\nu^{\varepsilon}_{1/2}(\cdot)
)
by the above convergence. By symmetry the conclusion of (
4.13
)
also holds if
|\xi_{0}^{[\varepsilon]}|=\infty
and so the proof of
(
4.12
) is complete.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

As noted in the above proof, a
cancellative finite range voter model perturbation is symmetric.

The key condition in the above is (
4.10
) which will imply that
a pair of nearby sites with opposite type can be found in sufficiently
large sets for large
t
. Such pairs are to be expected if there is
to be complete convergence with coexistence.

Assume now that for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
:

For
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
is a cancellative and monotone finite range voter model perturbation in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.

(4.14)

By Remarks
4.1
and
4.5
we may apply
Proposition
4.3
, and so for any initial condition

\xi_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
construct
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
,

{\underline{\xi}}^{[\varepsilon]}
,
\underline{\hat{\xi}}^{[\varepsilon]}
as
solutions of (
4.4
), (
4.5
) and
(
4.8
), respectively, with
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}=\xi_{0}
,
all on a common probability space such that

{\underline{\xi}}^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}\leq\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}\text{ and }\underline{\hat{\xi}}^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}\leq\widehat{\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}}\ \ \forall t\geq 0\ a.s.

(4.15)

We now assume that
M_{0}=KL
for natural numbers
K,L
chosen below.
As in Section
3.2
we often use
N\geq N(\varepsilon_{0})>e^{3}

satisfying
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{N}:=\frac{(\log N)^{3}}{N}
as our fundamental
parameter. Let

\underline{\xi}^{N}_{t}(x)=\underline{\xi}^{[\varepsilon_{N}]}_{Nt}(\sqrt{N}x),\ x\in{S_{N}},

(4.16)

and

\underline{X}^{N}_{t}=\frac{\log N}{N}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\underline{\xi}^{N}_{t}(x)\delta_{x},\text{ and }\underline{\hat{X}}^{N}_{t}=\frac{\log N}{N}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\hat{\underline{\xi}}^{N}_{t}(x)\delta_{x}.

(4.17)

The next condition is the key to ensure the survival of our oriented
percolation process. It specifies the values of
K,L
which are used
above to define our killed particle systems through
M_{0}=KL
. Note
that
{\underline{X}}^{N}_{t}(\mbox{\bf 1})={\underline{X}}^{N}_{t}((-M_{0},M_{0})^{2})<\infty.

There are
T^{\prime}>1
,

K,J^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
K>2
, and
L^{\prime}>3
, if

\displaystyle 0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0},\text{ and }I_{\pm e_{i}}=\pm 2L^{\prime}e_{i}+[-L^{\prime}+1,L^{\prime}-1]^{2},\text{ then for }L=\lfloor\sqrt{N}L^{\prime}\rfloor,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\underline{X}^{N}_{0}([-L^{\prime},L^{\prime}]^{2})\geq J^{\prime}\text{ implies }P(\underline{X}_{T^{\prime}}^{N}(I_{e})\geq J^{\prime}\text{ for all }e\in\{\pm e_{i},i=1,2\})\geq 1-6^{-5(2K+1)^{3}}.

Recall that if the conclusion of Theorem
4.4
holds we say
for
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}
the complete convergence theorem with coexistence
(CCT) holds for
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
. We use this terminology going forward.

Theorem 4.6
.

Assume for
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,

\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
is a cancellative and monotone finite range voter model
perturbation in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
satisfying (
4
). There is an

\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
, the complete convergence
theorem with coexistence (CCT) holds for
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
.

Proof.

We follow the general approach used in Section 6 of
[
13
]
for
the
d=2
Lotka-Volterra model. From Theorem
4.4
it
suffices to establish (
4.10
) and (
4.11
), as well as the
fact that
\beta_{\infty}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0})>0
for initial conditions
distinct from
{\bf 0}
and
{\bf 1}
(we have suppressed the
dependence on
\varepsilon
in
\beta_{\infty}
). Remark
4.5
shows
that
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
is symmetric. Therefore, we may use
Proposition
4.3
(c) to see that (
4
) implies
that,

\displaystyle\underline{X}^{N}_{0}([-L^{\prime},L^{\prime}]^{2})\geq J^{\prime}\text{
and }\underline{\hat{X}}^{N}_{0}([-L^{\prime},L^{\prime}]^{2})\geq J^{\prime}\text{ imply }

(4.19)

\displaystyle\qquad P(\underline{X}_{T^{\prime}}^{N}(I_{\pm e_{i}})\geq J^{\prime}\text{ and
}\underline{\hat{X}}_{T^{\prime}}^{N}(I_{\pm e_{i}})\geq J^{\prime}\text{ for }i=1,2)\geq 1-2\cdot 6^{-5(2K+1)^{3}}.

To undo the scaling, recall
L=\lfloor\sqrt{N}L^{\prime}\rfloor
and let

J=\frac{N}{\log N}J^{\prime}
,
T=NT^{\prime}
and set
\tilde{I}_{\pm e_{i}}=\pm 2Le_{i}+[-L,L]^{2}
,
i=1,2
. In order to use Theorem 4.3 of
[
16
]

we introduce a set
H\subset\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
of “happy” configurations
and a good event
G_{\xi_{0}}=G_{\xi_{0}}(\varepsilon)
in our probability space
for each initial condition
\xi_{0}
. We let

H=\{\xi\in\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}:\,\xi([-L,L]^{2})\geq J\text{ and }\widehat{\xi}([-L,L]^{2})\geq J\},

(4.20)

and

G_{\xi_{0}}=\{{\underline{\xi}}^{[\varepsilon]}_{T}(\tilde{I}_{\pm e_{i}})\geq J,\ \hat{\underline{\xi}}^{[\varepsilon]}_{T}(\tilde{I}_{\pm e_{i}})\geq J\text{ for
}i=1,2\},

(4.21)

where
\xi_{0}
is the initial condition for
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
, so that

\underline{\xi}^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}(x)=1_{I^{\prime}}(x)\xi_{0}(x)
and

\hat{{\underline{\xi}}}^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}(x)=1_{I^{\prime}}(x)\widehat{\xi}_{0}(x)
. Recall here
that
I^{\prime}=(-KL,KL)^{2}
. For
z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
,
\sigma_{z}:\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}\to\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
is the translation map,

\sigma_{z}(\xi)(x)=\sigma(x+z)
. Note that:

(i)
G_{\xi_{0}}
is

{\mathcal{G}}(I^{\prime}\times[0,T])
-measurable for each
\xi_{0}
.

(ii)
If
\xi_{0}\in H
, then on
G_{\xi_{0}}
,

\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{T}\in\sigma_{2Le}(H)
for all
e\in\{\pm e_{1},\pm e_{2}\}
.

(iii)
For any
\xi_{0}\in H
,

P(G_{\xi_{0}})\geq 1-2\cdot 6^{-5(2K+1)^{3}}:=1-\gamma^{\prime}
.

Properties (i) and (ii) are clear from the definitions and
the orderings in (
4.15
). Property (iii) follows from
(
4.19
), along with a bit of arithmetic on the rescaled
intervals to show
\sqrt{N}I_{\pm e_{i}}\subset\tilde{I}_{\pm e_{i}}
,
where
N\geq e^{3}
is used. Finally another bit of arithmetic shows that

(1-\gamma^{\prime})^{1/(2K+1)^{3}}>1-6^{-4}
, ensuring that (5.17) of

[
13
]
is valid. In this way we have established the set-up of
Lemma 5.2 of
[
13
]
and we can invoke the comparison with

2K
-dependent percolation from Theorem 4.3 of
[
16
]
, as
carried out in Section 5 of
[
13
]
. In particular, we may use
the proof of Lemma 5.3 of
[
13
]
to conclude that (
4.10
)
and (
4.11
) hold for
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
. Although the hypotheses of
that result require
d\geq 3
and
f^{\prime}(0)>0
for a solution to a
reaction diffusion equation (which is not defined in
d=2
), those
hypotheses are only used to establish the set-up in Lemma 5.2 of

[
13
]
, which we have just verified directly, essentially using
(
4
). The rest of the proof of Lemma 5.3 of
[
13
]
only
requires arguments for general voter model perturbations and, in
particular, uses its branching coalescing dual from Section 2 of

[
7
]
to bound some probabilities involving
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
. In
this way the proof of Lemma 5.3 of
[
13
]
gives us (
4.10
),
and the proof also gives (this is (5.31) of
[
13
]
)

\inf_{\xi\neq 0}P_{\xi}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{t}\neq{\bf 0}\ \forall\,t\geq 0)\geq\rho,

(4.22)

for some explicit
\rho>0
. After the proof of Lemma 5.3
in
[
13
]
, (
4.11
) is derived from
(
4.22
) using the above oriented percolation setting
and elementary properties of voter model perturbations,
which apply equally well in our setting.

To prove the last assertion on
\beta_{\infty}
, by (
1.3
)
it suffices to consider
0<|\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}|<\infty
or

0<|\widehat{\xi_{0}^{[\varepsilon]}}|<\infty
, and by the
0-1
-symmetry
(Remark
4.5
) we need only consider the first case. By
monotonicity and translation invariance we can take

\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}=\delta_{0}
. The fact that
{\bf 0}
is a trap implies

P_{\delta_{0}}(|\xi_{t}^{[\varepsilon]}|>0)
is non-increasing in
t
.
Therefore (
4.11
) easily implies
P_{\delta_{0}}(\tau_{{\bf 0}}=\infty)>0
. But
P_{\delta_{0}}(\tau_{{\bf 1}}=\infty)=1
by
(
1.3
), so we conclude
\beta_{\infty}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0})>0
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

The final paragraph in the above
proof applies equally well in
d\geq 3
to show that the conclusion of
Theorem 1.2 of
[
13
]
may be strengthened to include

\beta_{\infty}(\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0})>0
if
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}_{0}
is not
{\bf 0}

or
{\bf 1}
.

Remark 4.8
.

Theorems
4.4
and

4.6
hold without the finite range assumptions. That is, we
only require that
\{\xi^{[\varepsilon]},0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\}
is a

2
-dimensional voter model perturbation in the sense of
(1.10)–(1.15) of
[
13
]
(where the Hölder rate of convergence
in (1.14) of
[
13
]
(see (
1.14
)) is also weakened to
(
1.15
)). Indeed our proofs, and those quoted in
[
13
]
,
only require these conditions. To verify the key condition
(
4
), however, we will need to work with finite range voter
model perturbations and make a critical assumption on the parameter

\Theta_{2}+\Theta_{3}
from (
3.33
).

Theorem 4.9
.

Assume
\{\xi^{[\varepsilon]}:0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\}
is a monotone, asymptotically symmetric finite range voter
model perturbation in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
with
\Theta_{2}+\Theta_{3}>0
. Then
(
4
) holds, perhaps with a smaller choice of
\varepsilon_{0}>0
.

We give the proof in Section
10
. It
will follow from Theorem
1.15
, our weak convergence
result to super-Brownian motion with drift
\Theta_{2}+\Theta_{3}
.

We are ready for the proof of our main result,
Theorem
1.9
, a general complete convergence theorem for
monotone cancellative finite range voter model perturbations.

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.
By
Theorem
4.6
it suffices to establish (
4
), perhaps
with a smaller
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. By Remark
4.5
,
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
is
symmetric and so in particular is asymptotically symmetric by
Remark
3.6
. The latter Remark also shows that
r^{a}=0
and
therefore
\Theta_{2}=0
. Hence
\Theta_{3}+\Theta_{2}=\Theta_{3}>0
(by
hypothesis), and so Theorem
4.9
gives (
4
), and we
are done. ∎

To apply Theorem
1.9
it would be useful to have a
general, and checkable, sufficient condition for
\Theta_{3}>0
, which
would also apply to the
q
-voter model, and so establish
Theorem
1.1
for
d=2
as a special case. This is the goal of
the next section.

5
Positivity of the drift and the Proof of Theorem
1.1

for
d
=2

To establish a sufficient condition for

\Theta_{3}>0
in Theorem
1.9
, it will be convenient to
first work in a more general setting with any
general

neighbourhood
{\mathcal{N}}
(recall from Section
3.1
that
{\mathcal{N}}
is
finite non-empty subset of
Z^{d}\setminus\{0\}
) and
d\geq 2
. We
consider a strictly subadditive map
r:\{A:A\subset{\mathcal{N}}\}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. This
means that

r(A\cup B)<r(A)+r(B)\text{ for all
non-empty disjoint }A,B\subset{\mathcal{N}}.

(5.1)

By induction on
n
this implies that for non empty
disjoint sets
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
in
{\mathcal{N}}
,

r(\cup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}r(A_{i}),\ \ \text{where strict inequality holds if $n>1$}.

(5.2)

Later we will want to consider a finite range voter model perturbation
and take
r=r^{s}
. Assume for now that
d=2
. To motivate the above
definition recall from (
3.42
) that for the
q
-voter model
in
d=2
we have
r^{s}(A)=r_{|A|}
, where
r_{\ell}
are as in
(
1.20
). We saw in Section
1.4
(recall
(
1.24
)) that

r_{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}<r_{\ell_{1}}+r_{\ell_{2}}\text{ for
$0<\ell_{i}$, and $\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\leq|{\mathcal{N}}|$}.

(5.3)

and so

\text{for the $2$-dimensional
$q$-voter model, $r^{s}$ is strictly subadditive}.

(5.4)

Return now to our earlier setting with
d\geq 2
and general
{\mathcal{N}}
. If

\pi
is a partition of
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
,
[0]
denotes the cell of
\pi

containing
0
and
|\pi|
is the cardinality of
\pi
. We assume
(
5.1
) and that all sets in a partition are non-empty
throughout this section.

Lemma 5.1
.

If
\pi
is a fixed partition of
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
, then

\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)1(A\in\pi)\geq\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)1(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A=[0]).

(5.5)

The inequality is strict if
|\pi|>2
.

Proof.

The left-hand side of (
5.5
) trivially is

1(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0]\neq\emptyset)\sum_{\emptyset\neq A}r(A)1(A\in\pi,A\subset\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0]),

while the right-hand side equals

r(|\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0])1(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0]\neq\emptyset).

So to prove the result we may assume
\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0]\neq\emptyset
, or equivalently
|\pi|>1
. Using

\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0]=\cup_{A\in\pi,A\subset\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0]}A,

and the subadditivity (
5.2
), we have

r(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0])\leq\sum_{\emptyset\neq A}r(A)1(A\in\pi,A\subset\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0]),

thus giving (
5.5
). If
|\pi|=2
there is equality in the above,
and by (
5.2
) there is strict inequality if
|\pi|>2
.
∎

As an immediate consequence we have:

Lemma 5.2
.

If
\pi
is a random
partition of
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
such that

P(|\pi|>2)>0,

(5.6)

then

E\Bigl(\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)1(A\in\pi)\Bigr)>E\Bigl(\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)1(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A=[0])\Bigr).

(5.7)

Returning to our general
r
in
d=2
, and recalling the definitions
of
\Theta^{\pm}(A)
from (
1.10
), we define

\Theta_{3}=\Theta_{3}(r)
by

\Theta_{3}=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)\Theta^{+}(A)-\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)\Theta^{-}(A):=\Theta^{+}_{3}-\Theta^{-}_{3}.

(5.8)

Note that this agrees with our earlier definition of
\Theta_{3}
in
(
3.33
) if
r=r^{s}
for the finite range voter perturbations
in Section
3.2
. To use the above to show the positivity of

\Theta_{3}
in (
5.8
), recall
K_{3}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
from
Proposition
1.6
and the notation
{\mathcal{P}}_{k}(\Gamma)
and

{\mathcal{P}}(\Gamma)
from Section
1.3
. Recall also that
(
5.1
) is still in force and the
q
-voter drift
\Theta

in (
1.22
) corresponds to the special case
r(A)=r_{|A|}
with

r_{\ell}
as in (
1.20
).

Corollary 5.3
.

If
d=2
, then

\Theta_{3}>0
, and, in particular,
\Theta
in (
1.22
)
is also strictly positive.

Proof.

Let

\kappa=\sum_{\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{3}(\bar{\mathcal{N}})}K_{3}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})>0
,
where the sum is over sets, not ordered triples, and the
positivity is clear by
|\bar{\mathcal{N}}|\geq 5
(see (
1.2
)).
Define a random partition in
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
by

P(\pi=\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\})=K_{3}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})/\kappa.

Both are
well-defined by the symmetry of
K_{3}
, and (
5.6
)
holds because
|\pi|=3
a.s. If
A
is a non-empty subset
of
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
, then

P(A\in\pi)=\sum_{\{A_{1},A_{2}\}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)}P(\pi=\{A,A_{1},A_{2}\})=\sum_{\{A_{1},A_{2}\}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)}K_{3}(A,A_{1},A_{2})/\kappa.

(5.9)

Therefore from (
5.8
)

\displaystyle\Theta_{3}^{+}:=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)\Theta^{+}(A)=\kappa\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)P(A\in\pi)=\kappa E\Bigl(\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)1(A\in\pi)\Bigr).

(5.10)

Similarly, apply (
5.9
) with

\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A
in place of
A
to see that

\Theta_{3}^{-}:=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)\Theta^{-}(A)=\kappa\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)P(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A\in\pi)=\kappa E\Bigl(\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)1(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A\in\pi)\Bigr).

Note that for
A\subset{\mathcal{N}}
we have

0\in\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A
, and so
\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A\in\pi

iff
[0]=\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A
. This shows that the above
implies

\Theta_{3}^{-}=\kappa E\Bigl(\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)1(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A=[0])\Bigr).

(5.11)

So by Lemma
5.2
,
(
5.10
) and (
5.11
) we have

\Theta_{3}^{+}>\Theta_{3}^{-}
, and therefore,
\Theta_{3}>0
. As
noted above, the positivity of
\Theta
in (
1.22
)
follows from the special case
r(A)=r_{|A|}
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

If
r(A)=|A|
(not strictly
subadditive!) one easily sees that equality holds in
Lemmas
5.1
and
5.2
, the latter
without any condition on
\pi
. The above proof then shows
that

\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}|A|(\Theta^{+}(A)-\Theta^{-}(A))=0.

(5.12)

This identity simplified some of the formulae
for
\Theta_{3}
in the examples of Section
3.2
.
If
r(A)=1
for all
A\subset{\mathcal{N}}
(which is strictly
subadditive), then from (
5.10
) we have

\Theta_{3}^{+}=2\kappa
because there are exactly two subsets
of
{\mathcal{N}}
in
\pi
corresponding to the two sets in
\pi

other than
[0]
. Similarly from (
5.11
) we
get that
\Theta_{3}^{-}=\kappa
because there is exactly one
subset of
{\mathcal{N}}
whose complement in
\bar{\mathcal{N}}
is
[0]
,
namely
\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus[0]
.
\pi
containing
0
. Therefore

\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}(\Theta^{+}(A)-\Theta^{-}(A))=\kappa=\sum_{\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{3}(\bar{\mathcal{N}})}K_{3}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})=\lim_{t\to\infty}(\log t)^{3}\hat{P}(|B^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{t}|=3)>0.

(5.13)

The last equality follows easily from the definition of

K_{3}
by decomposing
\hat{P}(|B^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}}_{t}|=3)
into the
possible partitions induced by the sets of sites which have
coalesced at time
t
and taking limits.

The following corollary is immediate from
Theorem
1.9
and Corollary
5.3
. It
represents our simplest criteria for a CCT to hold in
d=2
.

Corollary 5.5
.

Assume
d=2
,

\{\xi^{[\varepsilon]}:0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\}
satisfies (
4.14
)
and
r^{s}
, given by (
1.18
), is strictly
subadditive (i.e., (
5.1
) holds for

r=r^{s}
). Then
\Theta_{3}>0
and there is an
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such
that for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
, the complete convergence
theorem with coexistence (CCT) holds for
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
.

To illustrate the use of the Corollary, we
first show how it quickly gives Theorem
1.1
(which
was already outlined in Section
1.4
).

Proof of Theorem
1.1
for
d=2
.
We
apply Corollary
5.5
above with
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}

the
(1-\varepsilon)
-voter model,
\xi^{(1-\varepsilon)}
. The
cancellative property for
|{\mathcal{N}}|\leq 8
is shown in
Lemma
3.3
for
\varepsilon
small enough, and the finite
range voter perturbation property is established in
Example
3.8
. The monotonicity of any
q
-voter model
is elementary (recall (
1.4
)). Strict
subadditivity of
r^{s}
was already noted in (
5.4
)
and so Corollary
5.5
gives the result. ∎

Recall from the examples at the end of Section
3.2

that for the Lotka-Volterra models, affine voter models and
geometric voter models, we have for non-empty
A\subset{\mathcal{N}}
,
r^{s}(A)=-(|A|/|{\mathcal{N}}|)^{2}
,

r^{s}(A)=-\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}|}+1
, and

r^{s}(A)=\frac{|A|(|{\mathcal{N}}|-|A|)}{2|{\mathcal{N}}|}
, respectively. All of
these asymptotic rate functions are strictly subadditive, as
one can easily check. Condition (
4.14
) was verified
for all of these models in Examples
3.9
-
3.11
,
where for the Lotka-Volterra model we take

\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}\geq 1/2
. The following theorems are then
also immediate consequences of
Corollary
5.5
. The first is the
d=2
case of
Theorem 1.1 of
[
13
]
which helped motivate the general
result here.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
d=2
, let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a neighbourhood, and let
LV(
\alpha
) denote the Lotka-Volterra model with parameters

\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha
. Then
\Theta^{\texttt{lv}}_{3}>0
and there
is an
\alpha_{c}\in(0,1)
such that for all

\alpha\in(\alpha_{c},1)
, the complete convergence theorem
with coexistence holds for LV(
\alpha
).

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
d=2
, let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a
neighbourhood, and let AV(
\alpha
) denote the affine voter
model with parameter
\alpha
. Then
\Theta^{\texttt{av}}_{3}>0
and
there is an
\alpha_{c}\in(0,1)
such that for all

\alpha\in(\alpha_{c},1)
, the complete convergence theorem
with coexistence holds for AV(
\alpha
).

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
d=2
, let
{\mathcal{N}}
be a neighbourhood, and let
GV(
\theta
) denote the geometric voter model with parameter

\theta
. Then
\Theta^{\texttt{gv}}_{3}>0
and there is a
\theta_{c}\in(0,1)
such that for all

\theta\in(\theta_{c},1)
, the complete convergence theorem
with coexistence holds for GV(
\theta
).

Finally, we give the promised direct proof of
f^{\prime}(0)>0
for
the
q
voter model and
d\geq 3
. Recall that in this case

r_{\ell}
is as in (
1.20
),

c^{*}(x,\xi)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{|{\mathcal{N}}|}r_{l}\Big(\widehat{\xi}(x)1\{n_{1}(x,\xi)=\ell\}+\xi(x)1\{n_{0}(x,\xi)=\ell\}\Big),

(5.14)

and
f
is given by
(
3.51
)
.

Proposition 5.9
.

(
[
4
]
) Assume

d\geq 3
and
{\mathcal{N}}
is a fixed neighbourhood. Then

f^{\prime}(0)>0
.

Proof.

It follows from (
3.51
) and (
5.14
)
that

\displaystyle f(u)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{\ell=1}^{|{\mathcal{N}}|}r_{\ell}\widehat{\xi}(0)1(n_{1}(0,\xi)=\ell)\Big\rangle_{u}-\Big\langle\sum_{\ell=1}^{|{\mathcal{N}}|}r_{\ell}\xi(0)1(n_{0}(0,\xi)=\ell)\Big\rangle_{u}

(5.15)

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=f_{1}(u)-f_{0}(u).

Let
\{B^{x}:x\in\bar{\mathcal{N}}\}
be the system of
coalescing random walks introduced in
Section
1.3
under
\hat{P}
, but now in
dimension
d\geq 3
, and let
\{\xi^{u}_{0}(x):x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\}
have
Bernoulli product measure with density
u\in[0,1]
. Let

\pi_{t}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathcal{N}})
be the random partition determined
by the coalescing random walks
\{B^{x}_{t},x\in\bar{\mathcal{N}}\}
using
the equivalence relation
x\sim_{t}y
iff

\sigma(x,y)=\inf\{u:B^{x}_{s}=B^{y}_{u}\}\leq t
, and let

\pi_{\infty}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\pi_{t}
. In this way

x\sim_{\infty}y
iff
\sigma(x,y)<\infty
is the associated
equivalence relation. If
A\subset\bar{\mathcal{N}}
and

T\in[0,\infty]
, let

[A]_{T}=\{\lambda\in\pi_{T}:\lambda\cap A\neq\emptyset\},

and, abusing this notation slightly, write
[x]_{T}
for the
cell of
\pi_{T}
containing
x
. Use the duality between the
voter model and coalescing random walk to see that

\displaystyle f_{1}(u)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}1(\xi|_{A}=1,\xi|_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A}=0)\Big\rangle_{u}

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\to\infty}\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}\sum_{i=1}^{|A|}\sum_{j=1}^{|{\mathcal{N}}|+1-|A|}\hat{P}(B^{A}_{T}\subset\{x:\xi_{0}^{u}(x)=1\},|B^{A}_{T}|=i,

\displaystyle\phantom{=\lim_{T\to\infty}\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}\sum_{i=1}^{|A|}\sum_{j=1}^{|{\mathcal{N}}|+1-|A|}\hat{P}(}B_{T}^{\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A}\subset\{x:\xi_{0}^{u}(x)=0\},|B^{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A}_{T}|=j)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}\sum_{i=1}^{|A|}\sum_{j=1}^{|{\mathcal{N}}|+1-|A|}\lim_{T\to\infty}\hat{P}(\sigma(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)>T,\ |[A]_{T}|=i,\ |[\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A]_{T}|=j)u^{i}(1-u)^{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}\sum_{i=1}^{|A|}\sum_{j=1}^{|{\mathcal{N}}|+1-|A|}\hat{P}(\sigma(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)=\infty,\ |[A]_{\infty}|=i,\ |[\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A]_{\infty}|=j)u^{i}(1-u)^{j}.

(5.16)

Differentiate the above at
u=0
and so conclude that

f_{1}^{\prime}(0)=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}\hat{P}(\sigma(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)=\infty,\ |[A]_{\infty}|=1).

Note that
\sigma(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)=\infty
iff
A
is a
union of cells in
\pi_{\infty}
and so

(\sigma(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)=\infty\text{ and
}|[A]_{\infty}|=1)\iff A\in\pi_{\infty}.

The above
expression for
f_{1}^{\prime}(0)
now becomes
r_{0}=r_{|{\mathcal{N}}|}=0
)

f_{1}^{\prime}(0)=\hat{E}\Bigl(\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}1(A\in\pi_{\infty})\Bigr).

(5.17)

In a similar way to (
5.16
), we get

\displaystyle f_{0}(u)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}1(\xi|_{A}=0,\xi_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A}=1)\Big\rangle_{u}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}\sum_{i=1}^{|A|}\sum_{j=1}^{|{\mathcal{N}}|+1-|A|}\hat{P}(\sigma(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)=\infty,\ |[A]_{\infty}|=i,\ |[\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A]_{\infty}|=j)(1-u)^{i}u^{j}.

Differentiating at
u=0
we get

\displaystyle f_{0}^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}\hat{P}(\sigma(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)=\infty,\ |[\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A]_{\infty}|=1)

\displaystyle=\hat{E}\Bigl(\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r_{|A|}1(\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A=[0]_{\infty})\Bigr).

(5.18)

For the last, note that for
A\subset{\mathcal{N}}
, the event inside
the
\hat{P}
is precisely
\{\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A=[0]_{\infty}\}
.

Note that
\hat{P}(|\pi_{\infty}|=|{\mathcal{N}}|+1)>0
for
d\geq 3
as
there is positive probability none of the walks coalesce,
and so (
5.6
) holds. We now may apply
Lemma
5.2
to conclude from (
5.17
) and
(
5.18
) that
f^{\prime}_{1}(0)>f^{\prime}_{0}(0)
, and so
f^{\prime}(0)>0
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

The above proof applies equally well to any

\{r_{\ell}:1\leq\ell\leq|{\mathcal{N}}|\}
satisfying (
5.3
).
In fact, with only notational changes, it applies to
r
as
in (
5.1
) where
f
is given by (
3.51
) and

c^{*}(x,\xi)=\widehat{\xi}(0)\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)1(\xi|_{\mathcal{N}}=1_{A})+\xi(0)\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}r(A)1(\xi|_{\mathcal{N}}=1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A}).

(5.19)

6
A general convergence theorem to super-Brownian motion in two dimensions

In Section
4
we extended the SDE
construction of our particle systems to allow for infinite
initial conditions and also simultaneously deal with

{\widehat{\xi}^{[\varepsilon]}}
. In this setting we have no need to deal
with these extensions and so no longer require monotonicity
or have to deal with the equation (
4.7
) for

\widehat{\xi}
. Assume the walk kernel,
p
, is as in the beginning
of Section
1.4
and

\{\xi^{[\varepsilon]}:0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\}
is an asymptotically
symmetric finite range voter model perturbation as in
Section
3.2
. This assumption will be in force
throughout the rest of this work. We continue to use
notation from Section
3.2
and, as in that Section,

N\geq N(\varepsilon_{0})>e^{3}
is our fundamental parameter where

\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{N}=(\log N)^{3}/N
. Recall from (
3.23
)
that the rescaled voter model perturbation,
\xi^{N}
in
(
3.19
) has rate function

\displaystyle c^{N}(x,\xi^{(N)})=Nc^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi^{(N)})+(\log N)c^{N,a}(x,\xi^{(N)})+

\displaystyle(\log N)^{3}c^{N,s}(x,\xi^{(N)}),

(6.1)

\displaystyle x\in{S_{N}},\ \xi^{(N)}\in\{0,1\}^{S_{N}},

where
c^{N,s}
and
c^{N,a}
are as in
(
3.20
) and (
3.21
), respectively. Assume
throughout that
|\xi^{N}_{0}|<\infty
.

By Remark
4.1
we can construct
\xi^{N}
, as in
Section
4
, as the unique

({\mathcal{F}}^{N}_{t})
-adapted solution of

\xi^{N}_{t}(x)=\xi^{N}_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int(1-\xi^{N}_{s-}(x))1(u\leq c^{N}(x,\xi^{N}_{s-}))N^{x,0}(ds,du)\\
-\int_{0}^{t}\int\xi^{N}_{s-}(x)1(u\leq c^{N}(x,\xi^{N}_{s-}))N^{x,1}(ds,du),\quad t\geq 0,\ x\in{S_{N}}.

(6.2)

Then
\xi^{N}
is the unique Feller process
associated with the rate function
c^{N}(x,\xi)
. Here

\{N^{x,i},x\in{S_{N}},i=0,1\}
are independent Poisson point
processes on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
with intensity
ds\times du

and
\{{\mathcal{F}}^{N}_{t},t\geq 0\}
is the natural right-continuous
filtration generated by these point processes.

We again use this setting to couple spin-flip systems but
now in a different manner from Section
4
.
If
\bar{c}^{N}(x,\xi)
is a rate function, also satisfying
(
4.2
) and (
4.3
),
such that for
\xi\leq\bar{\xi}
,

\displaystyle\bar{c}^{N}(x,\bar{\xi})

\displaystyle\geq c^{N}(x,\xi)\text{ if
}\xi(x)=\bar{\xi}(x)=0,

(6.3)

\displaystyle\bar{c}^{N}(x,\bar{\xi})

\displaystyle\leq c^{N}(x,\xi)\text{ if }\xi(x)=\bar{\xi}(x)=1,

and
\bar{\xi}^{N}_{t}
is constructed as in
(
6.2
) using
\bar{c}^{N}(x,\xi)
, then

\bar{\xi}^{N}_{0}\geq\xi^{N}_{0}\text{ implies }\bar{\xi}^{N}_{t}\geq\xi^{N}_{t}\text{ for
all $t\geq 0$}.

(6.4)

In Proposition 2.1 of
[
11
]
this is
proved under monotonicity of
\xi^{N}
when
\xi^{N}
is a
killed version of
\bar{\xi}^{N}
, but the same elementary
argument applies without monotonicity under
(
6.3
).

As in (
1.32
), define the measure-valued
process associated with
\xi_{t}^{N}(x),\ x\in{S_{N}}
by

X^{N}_{t}=(1/N^{\prime})\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\xi^{N}_{t}(x)\delta_{x}.

(6.5)

We use the SDE (
6.2
) to see that
X^{N}
satisfies
a martingale problem reminiscent of that of SBM. Introduce
the scaled probability kernel

p_{N}(x)=p(x\sqrt{N}),\ \ x\in{S_{N}}.

(6.6)

Unless otherwise indicated, assume
\Phi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
is such that

\overset{\centerdot}{\Phi}:=\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}\in C_{b}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. Define

A_{N}\Phi(s,x)=\sum_{y\in S_{N}}Np_{N}(y-x)\left(\Phi(s,y)-\Phi(s,x)\right),

and

D^{N,1}_{t}(\Phi)=\int_{0}^{t}X^{N}_{s}\big(A_{N}\Phi(s,\cdot)+\dot{\Phi}(s,\cdot)\big)ds.

Introduce

\ell^{(j)}_{N}=\begin{cases}\log N&\text{if }j=2,\\
(\log N)^{3}&\text{if }j=3.\end{cases}

To be consistent with the notation in
[
9
]
for

x\in{S_{N}}
and
\xi\in\{0,1\}^{{S_{N}}}
we define

\displaystyle d^{N,2}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\xi}(x)c^{N,a}(x,\xi)

(6.7)

\displaystyle d^{N,3}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\xi}(x)c^{N,s}(x,\xi)-\xi(x)c^{N,s}(x,\xi)

and for
j=2,3
,

\displaystyle d^{N,j}(s,\xi,\Phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell^{(j)}_{N}}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\Phi(s,x)d^{N,j}(x,\xi),

(6.8)

\displaystyle D^{N,j}_{t}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}d^{N,j}(s,\xi^{N}_{s},\Phi)ds.

(6.9)

One may then use the stochastic calculus for Poisson
integrals and integration by parts to rewrite

\xi^{N}_{t}(x)\Phi(t,x)
, just as in Propositions 2.2 and 2.3
in
[
10
]
, to see that

X_{t}^{N}(\Phi(t,\cdot))=X^{N}_{0}(\Phi(0,\cdot))+D_{t}^{N,1}(\Phi)+D_{t}^{N,2}(\Phi)+D_{t}^{N,3}(\Phi)+M_{t}^{N}(\Phi),

(6.10)

where
M^{N}_{t}(\Phi)
is a square integrable

(\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t})
-martingale
with previsible square function

\langle M^{N}(\Phi)\rangle_{t}=\langle M^{N}(\Phi)\rangle_{1,t}+\langle M^{N}(\Phi)\rangle_{2,t},

(6.11)

with

\displaystyle\langle M^{N}(\Phi)\rangle_{1,t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\log N}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\Phi(s,x)^{2}c^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi^{N}_{s})ds,

(6.12)

\displaystyle\langle M^{N}(\Phi)\rangle_{2,t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{{N^{\prime}}^{2}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\Phi(s.x)^{2}[\ell^{(2)}_{N}c^{N,a}(x,\xi^{N}_{s})+\ell_{N}^{(3)}c^{N,s}(x,\xi^{N}_{s})]\,ds.

Note that in spite of the suggestive notation the last term
may in fact be negative. The above three displays are
reminiscent of the martingale problem (MP) for a
super-Brownian motion in Section
1.5
. In the
next two sections we will take term by term limits in
(
6.10
) to establish Theorem
1.15
, our
general weak convergence result. We restate it below for
convenience. Recall that
\sigma^{2}
is as in (
3.13
)
and
\Theta_{2},\Theta_{3}
are defined in (
3.33
).

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume

\{\xi^{[\varepsilon]}:0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\}
is an asymptotically
symmetric finite range voter model perturbation on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
If
X_{0}^{N}\to X_{0}
in
{\mathcal{M}_{F}}
, then

X^{N}\Rightarrow\text{SBM}(X_{0},4\pi\sigma^{2},\sigma^{2},\Theta_{2}+\Theta_{3})\text{ in the Skorokhod space $D({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathcal{M}_{F}})$ as }N\to\infty.

In Remark
1.16
we showed how
Theorem
1.11
, the rescaled limit theorem for

2
-dimensional
q
-voter models, follows from the above.
We now describe a number of other corollaries, all of course
in two dimensions.

Example 6.2
.

(Lotka-Volterra Models). Recall
from Example
3.9
that the Lotka-Volterra Models
discussed there constituted an asymptotically symmetric
finite range voter model. The kernel
p
is
1_{\mathcal{N}}/|{\mathcal{N}}|

and so
\sigma^{2}
is as in (
1.1
). Recall also
that
\xi^{[\varepsilon_{N}]}
denotes a Lotka-Volterra model with
parameters
(\alpha^{N}_{1},\alpha^{N}_{2})
, where

\alpha_{i}^{N}=1-\varepsilon_{N}+\beta_{i}^{(\varepsilon_{N})}(\log(1/\varepsilon_{N}))^{-2}\varepsilon_{N}=1-\frac{(\log N)^{3}}{N}+\beta_{i}^{N}\frac{\log N}{N},

and

\beta^{N}_{i}\to\beta_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
as
N\to\infty
. Let

\xi^{N}_{t}(x)=\xi_{Nt}^{[\varepsilon_{N}]}(x\sqrt{N})\text{ for }x\in{S_{N}},\text{ and
}X_{t}^{N}=\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\xi^{N}_{t}(x)\delta_{x}.

(6.13)

If
X^{N}_{0}\to X_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}_{F}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
as
N\to\infty
, then
Theorem
6.1
implies that

X^{N}\Rightarrow\text{SBM}(X_{0},4\pi\sigma^{2},\sigma^{2},\Theta^{\texttt{lv}}_{2}+\Theta^{\texttt{lv}}_{3}),

where
\Theta_{i}^{\texttt{lv}}
are as in Example
3.9
. We can
write these drifts in another way. Let
e_{1},e_{2}
be iid rv’s
uniformly distributed over
{\mathcal{N}}
and, using the notation
from Section
1.3
, set

K=E(K_{3}(0,e_{1},e_{2})1(e_{1}\neq e_{2})),\ \text{ and }\gamma=E(K_{2}(\{e_{1},e_{2}\},\{0\})).

(6.14)

Then with a bit of work one can show that
\Theta_{3}^{\texttt{lv}}=K

and
\Theta_{2}^{\texttt{lv}}=(\beta_{0}-\beta_{1})\gamma
. For example, in
the proof of the latter, the summand
A
arising in

\Theta_{2}^{\texttt{lv}}
will be
[e_{1}]_{t}
(recall from the proof of
Proposition
5.9
this is the set of initial
conditions in
{\mathcal{N}}
that have coalesced with
e_{1}
by time

t
) and then let
t\to\infty
. In this way the drift of
the limiting SBM becomes
K+(\beta_{0}-\beta_{1})\gamma
. This
is the form of the limit derived in Theorem 1.5 of

[
9
]
whose proof will play an important role in the
derivation of Theorem
6.1
to come.

Example 6.3
.

(Affine Voter Models). In
Example
3.10
we showed the affine voter models are an
asymptotically symmetric voter model perturbation with
kernel
p(x)=1_{\mathcal{N}}(x)/|{\mathcal{N}}|
,
\sigma^{2}
as in
(
1.1
),
\Theta_{2}=0
, and

\Theta_{3}^{\texttt{av}}=\kappa=\lim_{t\to\infty}(\log t)^{3}\hat{P}(|B^{\mathcal{N}}_{t}|=3)>0
(recall (
5.13
)). Let

\xi^{[\varepsilon_{N}]}_{Nt}(x)
,
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
be an affine voter
model with parameter
\alpha=1-\varepsilon_{N}
, and define
\xi^{N}

and
X^{N}
as in (
6.13
). Theorem
6.1

implies that

\text{if }X^{N}_{0}\to X_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}_{F}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}),\text{ then }X^{N}\Rightarrow\text{SBM}(X_{0},4\pi\sigma^{2},\sigma^{2},\kappa).

Example 6.4
.

(Geometric Voter Models). By
Example
3.11
, the geometric voter models give an
asymptotic symmetric voter model perturbation with
p
and

\sigma^{2}
as above,
\Theta_{2}=0
and

\Theta^{\texttt{gv}}_{3}=\frac{|{\mathcal{N}}|}{2}\Theta_{3}^{\texttt{lv}}=\frac{|{\mathcal{N}}|}{2}K

where
K>0
is as in (
6.14
). Let
\xi^{[\varepsilon_{N}]}

be a geometric voter model with parameter
\theta=1-\varepsilon_{N}

and assume
\xi^{N}
and
X^{N}
are as in (
6.13
).
Theorem
6.1
implies that

\text{if }X^{N}_{0}\to X_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}_{F}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}),\text{ then }X^{N}\Rightarrow\text{SBM}(X_{0},4\pi\sigma^{2},\sigma^{2},\frac{|{\mathcal{N}}|}{2}K).

Remark 6.5
.

We have started with a finite
range voter model perturbation
\{c_{\varepsilon}:0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\}

and rescaled to obtain rates
c^{N}
as in
(
6.1
). At times it may be more natural to
start with the rescaled rates
c^{N}
for
N\geq N_{0}\geq e^{3}
.
Assume now that

\displaystyle c^{N}(x,\xi^{(N)})=Nc^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi^{(N)})+(\log N)c^{N,a,*}(x,\xi^{(N)})+

\displaystyle(\log N)^{3}c^{N,s,*}(x,\xi^{(N)}),

\displaystyle x\in{S_{N}},\ \xi^{(N)}\in\{0,1\}^{S_{N}},

where for some
{\mathbb{R}}
-valued functions

g_{i}^{N,a,*}
,
g^{N,s,*}
on
\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}}
,
i=0,1
,

c^{N,a,*}(x,\xi^{(N)})=\widehat{\xi}(x\sqrt{N})g_{1}^{N,a,*}(\xi|_{x\sqrt{N}+{\mathcal{N}}})+\xi(x\sqrt{N})g_{0}^{N,a,*}(\xi|_{x\sqrt{N}+{\mathcal{N}}}),

(6.15)

and

c^{N,s,*}(x,\xi^{(N)})=\widehat{\xi}(x\sqrt{N})g^{N,s,*}(\widehat{\xi}|_{x\sqrt{N}+{\mathcal{N}}})+\xi(x\sqrt{N})g^{N,s,*}(\xi|_{x\sqrt{N}+{\mathcal{N}}}).

(6.16)

We assume there are functions
g_{i}^{a,*}
,
g^{s,*}
on

\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}}
,
i=0,1
, such that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Bigl(\sum_{i=0}^{1}\|g_{i}^{a,N,*}-g_{i}^{a,*}\|_{\infty}\Bigl)+\|g^{s,N,*}-g^{s,*}\|_{\infty}=0,

and also that
{\bf 0}

and
{\bf 1}
are traps, that is, for all
N
and
x\in{S_{N}}
,

c^{N}(x,{\bf 0})=c^{N}(x,{\bf 1})=0
. This setting clearly
includes the
c^{N}
arising in (
6.1
), and in
fact appears to be more general since (
6.1
)
requires
\xi(x\sqrt{N})c^{N,a}(x,\xi^{(N)})=0
. To see that
it is in fact included in (
6.1
), define for

\xi\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{N}}
,

g_{0}^{\varepsilon_{N}}(\xi)=g^{N,s,*}(\xi)+(\log N)^{-2}g_{0}^{N,a,*}(\xi)\to g^{s,*}(\xi)\ \ \text{as
}N\to\infty,

(6.17)

and

g_{1}^{\varepsilon_{N}}(\xi)=g_{0}^{\varepsilon_{N}}(\widehat{\xi})+(\log N)^{-2}[g_{1}^{N,a,*}(\xi)-g_{0}^{N,a,*}(\widehat{\xi})]\to g^{s,*}(\widehat{\xi})\ \ \text{as }N\to\infty.

(6.18)

Then one easily checks that
c^{N}
is as in
(
6.1
) where
c^{N,s}
and
c^{N,a}
are given
by (
3.20
) and (
3.21
), respectively, in terms
of the
g^{\varepsilon_{N}}_{i}
given above. Moreover,

\displaystyle g^{a}(\xi):=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}(\log(1/\varepsilon_{N}))^{2}(g_{1}^{\varepsilon_{N}}(\xi)-g_{0}^{\varepsilon_{N}}(\widehat{\xi}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}(\log(1/\varepsilon_{N}))^{2}(\log N)^{-2}[g_{1}^{N,a,*}(\xi)-g_{0}^{N,a,*}(\widehat{\xi})]=g_{1}^{a,*}(\xi)-g_{0}^{a,*}(\widehat{\xi}).

Use
c^{N}
to define
c_{\varepsilon_{N}}
as in (
3.18
). Then

\{c_{\varepsilon}:0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}:=\varepsilon_{N_{0}}\}
is an
asymptotically symmetric voter model perturbation. Recalling
(
3.28
), for
A\subset{\mathcal{N}}
we have

r^{a}(A)=g^{a}(1_{A})=g_{1}^{a,*}(1_{A})-g_{0}^{a,*}(1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A}),

(6.19)

while (
6.17
) and (
3.30
) give

r^{s}(A)=\lim_{N\to\infty}g_{0}^{\varepsilon_{N}}(1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A})=g^{s,*}(1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A}).

(6.20)

If
\xi^{[\varepsilon]}
is the process with rate
c_{\varepsilon}
, then
(
3.18
) implies that

\xi^{N}_{t}(x)=\xi^{[\varepsilon_{N}]}_{N\cdot}(\sqrt{N}\cdot)

(
x\in{S_{N}}
) has rate function
c^{N}
. Therefore if
X^{N}
is
as in (
6.5
), we may apply Theorem
6.1

to conclude that
X^{N}\Rightarrow\text{SBM}(X_{0},4\pi\sigma^{2},\sigma^{2},\Theta_{2}+\Theta_{3})
,
where (by (
6.19
) and (
6.20
))

\Theta_{2}=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}(g^{a,*}_{1}(1_{A})-g_{0}^{a,*}(1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A}))K_{2}(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A),\ \Theta_{3}=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}g^{s,*}(1_{{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A})(\Theta^{+}(A)-\Theta^{-}(A)).

(6.21)

Example 6.6
.

As a specific example of the
above, recall the lower order weak limit theorem for
two-dimensional Lotka-Volterra models in
[
12
]
, where
now
\alpha^{N}_{i}=1+\frac{\log N}{N}\beta_{i}^{N}
and

\beta^{N}_{i}\to\beta_{i}
,
i=0,1
. We continue to assume

p(x)=1_{\mathcal{N}}/|{\mathcal{N}}|
. This led to rescaled rates (see (1.6) in

[
12
]
)

c^{N}(x,\xi^{(N)})=Nc^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi^{(N)})+\log N\Bigl[\widehat{\xi}(x\sqrt{N})\beta_{0}^{N}f_{1}(x\sqrt{N},\xi)^{2}+\xi(x\sqrt{N})\beta^{N}_{1}f_{0}(x\sqrt{N},\xi)^{2}\Bigr].

So comparing with (
6.15
) and
(
6.16
) above we have
c^{N,s,*}=g^{x,*}=0
, and

g_{1}^{a,*}(\xi)=\beta_{0}f_{1}(0,\xi)^{2}
and

g_{0}^{a,*}(\widehat{\xi})=\beta_{1}f_{0}(0,\widehat{\xi})^{2}=\beta_{1}f_{1}(x,\xi)^{2}
. So
(
6.21
) implies
\Theta_{3}=0
and

\Theta_{2}=(\beta_{0}-\beta_{1})\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}}\Bigl(\frac{|A|}{|{\mathcal{N}}|}\Bigr)^{2}K_{2}(A,\bar{\mathcal{N}}\setminus A)=\Theta_{2}^{\texttt{lv}}=(\beta_{0}-\beta_{1})\gamma,

where
\gamma
is
as in Example
6.2
, which also gives the above
expression for
\Theta_{2}^{\texttt{lv}}
. Therefore by
Remark
6.5
and Theorem
6.1
, if we
define
\xi^{N}
, and
X^{N}
as in Example
6.2
and

X^{N}_{0}\to X_{0}
, then

X^{N}\Rightarrow\text{SBM}(X_{0},4\pi\sigma^{2},\sigma^{2},(\beta_{0}-\beta_{1})\gamma).

This is Theorem 1.2 of
[
12
]
but with a seemingly
different parameter,
\gamma
, in place of the
\gamma^{*}
in

[
12
]
. It is, however, easy to use Proposition 2.2 of

[
12
]
and (
1.9
) with
n=2
to check that

\gamma^{*}=\gamma
.

7
Controlling the drift terms, and total mass bounds

The goal in this section (Proposition
7.16
) is to
show that for
j=2,3
, the drift terms
D^{N,j}_{t}(\Phi)

arising in (
6.10
) behave asymptotically like

\Theta_{j}\int_{0}^{t}X^{N}_{s}(\Phi_{s})\,ds
, where

\Phi_{s}(x)=\Phi(s,x)
. Use notation from the previous
section. In particular,
\xi^{N}
is as in (
3.19
)
with rate function,
c^{N}
, as in (
6.1
).

7.1
Small time comparison bounds

We begin with some elementary bounds on the drifts. Let

{\mathcal{N}}_{N}={\mathcal{N}}/\sqrt{N}
. By the definitions of

d^{N,j}(x,\xi)
, (
3.27
) and (
3.29
), we have

\displaystyle d^{N,2}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\xi}(x)\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,a}(\sqrt{N}A)1\{\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+A}\},

(7.1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,a}(\sqrt{N}A)1\{\xi|_{x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+A}\},

\displaystyle d^{N,3}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\xi}(x)\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A)1\{\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+A}\}-\xi(x)\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A)1\{\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\setminus A}\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A)1\{\xi|_{x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+A}\}-\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A)1\{\xi|_{x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\setminus A}\}.

Observe that at most one term in each of the above sums can
be nonzero, and this would require that
\xi(y)=1
for some

y\in x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}
. This implies that for
j=2,3
,

|d^{N,j}(x,\xi)|\leq\|r\|1\{\xi(y)=1\text{ for some }y\in x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\}\leq\|r\|\sum_{y\in x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}\xi(y),

(7.2)

and hence

|d^{N,j}(x,\xi)|\leq\|r\|,

(7.3)

and for
\Phi:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

|d^{N,j}(s,\xi,\Phi)|\leq\|r\||\Phi_{s}|_{\infty}|\bar{\mathcal{N}}|\frac{\ell^{(j)}_{N}}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\xi(x).

(7.4)

To control the drifts over small time intervals we will
condition
d^{N,2}(s,\xi^{N}_{s},\Phi)
and

d^{N,3}(s,\xi^{N}_{s},\Phi)
on
{\mathcal{F}}^{N}_{s-u_{N}}
for small
u_{N}

and compare this small time evolution of
\xi^{N}
with a
voter model, for which we can make explicit calculations
using duality.

Set
f_{i}^{(N)}(x,\xi)=\sum_{y\in{S_{N}}}p_{N}(y-x)1\{\xi(y)=i\}
,

n_{i}^{(N)}(x,\xi)=\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}1\{\xi(x+y)=i\}
,
\underline{p}=\min\{p(y):p(y)>0\}>0
, and introduce

w_{N}=1-\frac{\|r\|}{\underline{p}}\varepsilon_{N}\to 1\text{ as }N\to\infty.

(7.5)

We use the construction given in (
6.2
) with
other rate functions to provide a coupling of
\xi^{N}_{t}
with
some comparison processes. First, let

\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{t}\in\{0,1\}^{S_{N}}
be the rescaled voter model
defined as in (
6.2
) but using the rate function

Nw_{N}c^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi),\ x\in{S_{N}},\xi\in\{0,1\}^{S_{N}}.

Next,
define the 1-biased rate function

\displaystyle\bar{c}^{N,b}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=Nw_{N}c^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+\widehat{\xi}(x)\Bigl(2+{\underline{p}}^{-1}\Bigr)\|r\|(\log N)^{3}n^{(N)}_{1}(x,\xi).

(7.6)

Let

\bar{\xi}^{N}_{t}
be the 1-biased voter model constructed
with
\bar{c}^{N,b}

as in
(
6.2
).

We next verify (
6.3
) for
c^{N}
and
\bar{c}^{N,b}
, that is we will show that for
\xi\leq\bar{\xi}
,

\displaystyle{\bar{c}}^{N,b}(x,\bar{\xi})

\displaystyle\geq c^{N}(x,\xi)\text{ if }\xi(x)=\bar{\xi}(x)=0,

(7.7)

\displaystyle{\bar{c}}^{N,b}(x,\bar{\xi})

\displaystyle\leq c^{N}(x,\xi)\text{ if }\xi(x)=\bar{\xi}(x)=1.

First combine (
3.23
),
(
3.27
) and (
3.29
) to that for
x\in{S_{N}}
and

\xi\in\{0,1\}^{S_{N}}
,

\displaystyle c^{N}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=Nc^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+\widehat{\xi}(x)\Bigl[\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}(\log Nr^{N,a}(\sqrt{N}A)+(\log N)^{3}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A))1(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+A})\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad+\xi(x)\Bigl[\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}(\log N)^{3}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A)1(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\setminus A})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=Nw_{N}c^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+\widehat{\xi}(x)\Bigl[\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}(\log Nr^{N,a}(\sqrt{N}A)+(\log N)^{3}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A))1(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+A})

\displaystyle\phantom{=Nw_{N}c^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+\widehat{\xi}(x)\Bigl[\ \ }+N(1-w_{N})f_{1}^{(N)}(x,\xi)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad+\xi(x)\Bigl[\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}(\log N)^{3}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A)1(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\setminus A})+N(1-w_{N})f_{0}^{(N)}(x,\xi)\Bigr]

\displaystyle:=Nw_{N}c^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+\widehat{\xi}(x)\tilde{c}_{1}^{N}(x,\xi)+\xi(x)\tilde{c}_{0}^{N}(x,\xi).

(7.8)

If
\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\setminus A}
and
p(\sqrt{N}A)>0
, then

\frac{f_{0}^{(N)}(x,\xi)}{\underline{p}}=\sum_{y\in\sqrt{N}A}\frac{p(y)}{\underline{p}}\geq 1.

Use Lemma
3.7
and then the above to see that

\displaystyle\tilde{c}^{N}_{0}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N},p(\sqrt{N}A)>0}(\log N)^{3}(r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A)\wedge 0)1(\xi|_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=1_{x+{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\setminus A})+\frac{\|r\|}{\underline{p}}(\log N)^{3}f_{0}^{(N)}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\geq-(\log N)^{3}\|r\|\frac{f^{(N)}_{0}(x,\xi)}{\underline{p}}+\frac{\|r\|}{\underline{p}}(\log N)^{3}f_{0}^{(N)}(x,\xi)=0.

(7.9)

More simply we have

\tilde{c}^{N}_{1}(x,\xi)\leq\Bigl[2\|r\|(\log N)^{3}+\frac{\|r\|}{\underline{p}}(\log N)^{3}\Bigr]n_{1}^{(N)}(x,\xi).

(7.10)

Turning to (
7.7
), assume now that
\xi\leq\bar{\xi}
.
If
\xi(x)=\bar{\xi}(x)=1
, then by
(
7.1
),(
7.9
) and the monotonicity of the
voter model,

\displaystyle c^{N}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=Nw_{N}c^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)+\tilde{c}^{N}_{0}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\geq Nw_{N}c^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)\geq Nw_{N}c^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\bar{\xi})=\bar{c}^{N,b}(x,\bar{\xi}),

the last by (
7.6
). If
\xi(x)=\bar{\xi}(x)=0
,
then by (
7.1
) and (
7.10
),

\displaystyle c^{N}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=Nw_{N}f^{(N)}_{1}(x,\xi)+\tilde{c}^{N}_{1}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle\leq Nw_{N}f^{(N)}_{1}(x,\bar{\xi})+(2+{\underline{p}}^{-1})|r\|(\log N)^{3}n_{1}^{(N)}(x,\xi)=\bar{c}^{N,b}(x,\bar{\xi}),

where (
7.6
) is again used in
the last equality. This proves (
7.7
).

More simply (
7.7
) also holds if
c^{N}(x,\xi)
is
replaced with
w_{N}Nc^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(x,\xi)
. This is immediate
from the monotonicity of the voter model and
(
7.6
). Having verified (
6.3
) for
two pairs of processs we may apply the coupling result
(
6.4
) and conclude that if the three processes

\xi^{N},\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}},\bar{\xi}^{N}
have the same initial state

\xi^{N}_{0}
, then with probability one,

\xi^{N}_{t}\leq\bar{\xi}^{N}_{t},\text{ and }\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{t}\leq\bar{\xi}^{N}_{t}\text{ for
all }t\geq 0.

(7.11)

Use these processes to define the empirical processes of
one’s,
{\bar{X}}^{N}_{t}
and
X^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{t}
respectively, as in
(
6.5
). We will need to compare these processes
over small time periods. We assume
u_{N}
satisfies

\frac{C_{\ref{uNdef}}}{\sqrt{N}}\leq u_{N}\leq(\log N)^{-p}\text{ for some }C_{\ref{uNdef}}>0,p>6,

(7.12)

and recalling that as
N\geq e^{3}
, we have
u_{N}(\log N)^{3}<1
.

Lemma 7.1
.

For some universal

C_{\ref{masscmps}}
and all
N
, if

\xi^{N}_{0}=\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{0}=\xi^{N}_{0}
then

\displaystyle E\big[\bar{X}^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})-X^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})\big]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\ref{masscmps}}(\log N)^{3-p}X^{N}_{0}(\mbox{\bf 1}),

(7.13)

\displaystyle E\big[\bar{X}^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})-X^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})\big]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\ref{masscmps}}(\log N)^{3-p}X^{N}_{0}(\mbox{\bf 1}),

\displaystyle E\big[\big|X^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})-X^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})\big|\big]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\ref{masscmps}}(\log N)^{3-p}X^{N}_{0}(\mbox{\bf 1}).

Proof.

For the first inequality, by Lemma 4.1 in
[
10
]
,

E\bar{X}^{N}_{s}(\mbox{\bf 1})\leq e^{(2+{\underline{p}}^{-1})\|r\|(\log N)^{3}s}X^{N}_{0}(\mbox{\bf 1})
. Set

\Phi=\mbox{\bf 1}
in (
6.10
) to get

E[X_{t}^{N}(\mbox{\bf 1})]=X^{N}_{0}(\mbox{\bf 1})+\int_{0}^{t}E[d^{N,2}(s,\xi^{N}_{s},\mbox{\bf 1})+d^{N,3}(s,\xi^{N}_{s},\mbox{\bf 1})]\,ds,

where by (
7.4
),

E\big[|d^{N,2}(s,\xi^{N}_{s},\mbox{\bf 1})|+|d^{N,3}(s,\xi^{N}_{s},\mbox{\bf 1})|\big]\leq 2\|r\||{\mathcal{N}}|(\log N)^{3}E[X^{N}_{s}(\mbox{\bf 1})]
.
An elementary integration by parts now implies

E[X^{N}_{s}(\mbox{\bf 1})]\geq e^{-2\|r\||{\mathcal{N}}|(\log N)^{3}s}X_{0}^{N}(\mbox{\bf 1})
.
The above inequalities with
s=u_{N}
give the first
inequality. The second is even simpler since then

X^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}(\mbox{\bf 1})
is a martingale. The final inequality then
follows by the triangle inequality.
∎

Let
\Phi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and
\|\Phi\|_{\infty}
denote its sup norm. Define
|\Phi|_{\text{{Lip}}}
,
respectively
|\Phi|_{1/2,N}
, to be the smallest element in

[0,\infty]
such that

\begin{cases}|\Phi(s,x)-\Phi(s,y)|\leq|\Phi|_{\text{{Lip}}}|x-y|,\quad\forall s\in[0,T],x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\\
|\Phi(s-u_{N},x)-\Phi(s,x)|\leq|\Phi|_{1/2,N}\sqrt{u_{N}},\quad\forall s\in[u_{N},T],x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.\end{cases}

(7.14)

We will write
\|\Phi\|_{\text{{Lip}}}=\|\Phi\|_{\infty}+|\Phi|_{Lip}
,
\|\Phi\|_{1/2,N}=\|\Phi\|_{\infty}+|\Phi|_{1/2,N}
, and
\|\Phi\|_{N}=\|\Phi\|_{\infty}+|\Phi|_{Lip}+|\Phi|_{1/2,N}
. We also will abuse notation
slightly and write

\|\Phi_{s}\|_{\text{{Lip}}}=\|\Phi_{s}\|_{\infty}+|\Phi_{s}|_{\text{{Lip}}}
for the
usual Lipschitz norm of
x\to\Phi_{s}(x)=\Phi(s,x)
. Note
that since
u_{N}<\sqrt{u_{N}}
for
N\geq e^{3}
, we have

if, in addition,

\overset{\centerdot}{\Phi}\in C_{b}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, then

|\Phi|_{1/2,N}\leq\|\overset{\centerdot}{\Phi}\|_{\infty}

for all
N\geq e^{3}
.

(7.15)

We will often suppress the dependence on
N

in the above “norms”.

We claim that for
\xi\leq\eta
,
j=2,3
,

|d^{N,j}(x,\eta)-d^{N,j}(x,\xi)|\leq 2\|r\|\sum_{y\in x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}(\eta(y)-\xi(y)).

(7.16)

If
\xi\neq\eta
on
x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}
, then the right-hand side
is at least
2\|r\|
and so the above follows from
(
7.3
) and the triangle inequality. If

\xi|_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}=\eta|_{\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}
then the left-hand
side is zero, and so (
7.16
) is trivial.

Lemma 7.2
.

There is a constant

C_{\ref{dvmcomp}}>0
such that for
j=2,3
, all
T>0
,

\Phi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and all
s\in[0,T]
,

E_{\xi^{N}_{0}}\big[\big|d^{N,j}(s,\xi^{N}_{u_{N}},\Phi)-d^{N,j}(s,\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{u_{N}},\Phi)\big|\big]\leq C_{\ref{dvmcomp}}\|\Phi\|_{\infty}(\log N)^{6-p}X^{N}_{0}(\mbox{\bf 1}).

(7.17)

Proof.

Using (
7.16
) with
\eta=\bar{\xi}^{N}_{u_{N}}
and

\xi=\xi^{N}_{u_{N}}
and in (
6.8
) we see that

\displaystyle\Big|d^{N,j}(s,\bar{\xi}^{N}_{u_{N}},\Phi)-d^{N,j}(s,\xi^{N}_{u_{N}},\Phi)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|r\|\|\Phi\|_{\infty}\frac{\ell^{(j)}_{N}}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\Big[\sum_{y\in x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}\bar{\xi}^{N}_{u_{N}}(y)-\xi^{N}_{u_{N}}(y)\Big]

\displaystyle=2\|r\||\bar{\mathcal{N}}|\|\Phi\|_{\infty}\ell^{(j)}_{N}\big[\bar{X}^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})-X^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})\big].

Similarly,

\displaystyle\Big|d^{N,j}(s,\bar{\xi}^{N}_{u_{N}},\Phi)-d^{N,j}(s,\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{u_{N}},\Phi)\Big|\leq 2\|r\||\bar{\mathcal{N}}|\|\Phi\|_{\infty}\ell^{(j)}_{N}\big[\bar{X}^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})-X^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})\big].

It follows that the left-hand side of
(
7.17
) is bounded by

2\|r\||\bar{\mathcal{N}}|\|\Phi\|_{\infty}(\log N)^{3}E\big[(\bar{X}^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})-{X}^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1}))+(\bar{X}^{N}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})-{X}^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1}))\big].

Now Lemma
7.1
completes the proof.
∎

The next step is to consider

E[d^{N,j}(s,\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{u_{N}},\Phi)]
. Under a probability

\widehat{P}
, let
\{B^{N,x}_{t},x\in{S_{N}}\}
be a rate
w_{N}N
,
coalescing random walk system on
{S_{N}}
with jump kernel

p_{N}
and for
A\subset{S_{N}}
let
B^{N,A}_{t}=\cup_{x\in A}\{B^{N,x}_{t}\}.
For finite nonempty disjoint
A_{i}\subset{S_{N}}
define
\sigma^{N}(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})
and
\tau^{N}(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})
in the same way as
\sigma
and
\tau
are defined in
(
1.6
), but with
\{B^{N,x}:x\in{S_{N}}\}
in place of

\{B^{x}:x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\}
. Similarly define
\sigma^{N}(x_{1},\dots x_{n})
and
\tau^{N}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
for distinct
x_{i}
in

{S_{N}}
. If
x\in{S_{N}}
, then introduce

\sigma^{N}_{x}(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})=\sigma^{N}(x+A_{1},\dots,x+A_{n})
, and
similarly for
\tau_{x}^{N}(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})
.

The duality equation connecting the voter model

\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}
with the coalescing random walks

\{B^{N,x}_{t}\}
that we need is the following (see Section
III.4 of Lig85). For
x\in{S_{N}}
, finite disjoint sets

A,B\subset{S_{N}}
and
\xi^{N}_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{{S_{N}}}
,

E_{\xi^{N}_{0}}\Big[\prod_{a\in A}\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{t}(a)\,\prod_{b\in B}(1-\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{t}(b))\Big]=\widehat{E}\Big[\prod_{a\in A}\xi^{N}_{0}(B^{N,a}_{t})\prod_{b\in B}(1-\xi^{N}_{0}(B^{N,b}_{t}))\Big].

(7.18)

For
A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}
define

\displaystyle I^{N,+}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})

\displaystyle=\prod_{a\in A}\xi^{N}_{0}(B^{N,x+a}_{u_{N}})\prod_{b\in\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\setminus A}(1-\xi^{N}_{0}(B^{N,x+b}_{u_{N}}))

(7.19)

\displaystyle I^{N,-}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})

\displaystyle=\prod_{a\in\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}\setminus A}\xi^{N}_{0}(B^{N,x+a}_{u_{N}})\prod_{b\in A}(1-\xi^{N}_{0}(B^{N,x+b}_{u_{N}})).

With this notation, (
7.1
) and (
7.18
) imply
that

\displaystyle E_{\xi_{0}^{N}}\big(d^{N,2}(x,\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{u_{N}})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,a}\sqrt{N}A)\widehat{E}\big(I^{N,+}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})\big),

(7.20)

\displaystyle E_{\xi_{0}^{N}}\big(d^{N,3}(x,\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{u_{N}})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A)\Big[\widehat{E}\big(I^{N,+}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})\big)-\widehat{E}\big(I^{N,-}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})\big)\Big].

If we now define

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{N,2}(\xi^{N}_{0},u_{N},\Phi_{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell^{(2)}_{N}}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\Phi(s,x)\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,a}(\sqrt{N}A)\widehat{E}[I^{N,+}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})]

(7.21)

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{N,3}(\xi^{N}_{0},u_{N},\Phi_{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell^{(3)}_{N}}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\Phi(s,x)\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,s}(\sqrt{N}A)\Big(\widehat{E}[I^{N,+}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})]

(7.22)

\displaystyle\phantom{=\frac{\ell^{(3)}_{N}}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\Phi(s,x)\sum_{A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,s}({\sqrt{N}}A)\Big(}-\widehat{E}[I^{N,-}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})]\Big),

then for
j=2,3
,

E_{\xi^{N}_{0}}[d^{N,j}(s,\xi^{N,{\texttt{vm}}}_{u_{N}},\Phi)]=\hat{H}^{N,j}(\xi^{N}_{0},u_{N},\Phi_{s}).

(7.23)

Lemma 7.3
.

There is a constant
C_{\ref{d3H}}

such for
j=2,3
, all
T>0
,
\Phi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})

and all
s\in[u_{N},T]
,

\Big|E_{\xi^{N}_{0}}\big(d^{N,j}(s,\xi^{N}_{s},\Phi)|{\mathcal{F}}^{N}_{s-u_{N}}\big)-\hat{H}^{N,j}(\xi^{N}_{s-u_{N}},u_{N},\Phi_{s-u_{N}})\Big|\leq C_{\ref{d3H}}\|\Phi\|_{1/2,N}(\log N)^{3-\frac{p}{2}}X^{N}_{s-u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1}).

(7.24)

Proof.

By the Markov property, Lemma
7.2
and
(
7.23
), the left-hand side of (
7.24
) is
bounded above by

C_{\ref{dvmcomp}}\|\Phi\|_{\infty}(\log N)^{6-p}X^{N}_{s-u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1})+\Big|\hat{H}^{N,j}(\xi^{N}_{s-u_{N}},u_{N},\Phi_{s})-\hat{H}^{N,j}(\xi^{N}_{s-u_{N}},u_{N},\Phi_{s-u_{N}})\big]\Big|.

(7.25)

Use (
7.23
) and then (
7.2
) with the voter
duality (
7.18
) to see that the second term above is
bounded by

\displaystyle\|r\|\frac{\ell^{(j)}_{N}}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}

\displaystyle|\Phi(s,x)-\Phi(s-u_{N},x)|\widehat{E}\Big[\sum_{y\in\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}\xi^{N}_{s-u_{N}}(B^{N,x+y}_{u_{N}})\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\|r\||\Phi|_{1/2}\sqrt{u}_{N}\ell^{(j)}_{N}\sum_{y\in\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}\widehat{E}\Bigl[\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\xi^{N}_{s-u_{N}}(x+B^{N,y}_{u_{N}})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\|r\||\Phi|_{1/2}|\bar{\mathcal{N}}|(\log N)^{3-\frac{p}{2}}X^{N}_{s-u_{N}}(\mbox{\bf 1}).

Inserting this bound into (
7.25
) completes the proof.
∎

We next further decompose the
\hat{H}^{N,j}
. Consider

\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}
. If

I^{N,\pm}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})\neq 0
then necessarily
2\leq|B^{N,x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}_{u_{N}}|\leq|\bar{\mathcal{N}}|
, and by defining

I^{N,\pm}_{i}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})=I^{N,\pm}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})1\{|B^{N,x+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}_{u_{N}}|=i\},

(7.26)

we can write

I^{N,\pm}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})=\sum_{i=2}^{|\bar{\mathcal{N}}|}I^{N,\pm}_{i}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0}).

(7.27)

Letting

h^{N,\pm}_{i,j}(\xi^{N}_{0},u_{N},A,\Phi_{s})=\frac{\ell^{(j)}_{N}}{N^{\prime}}\sum_{x\in{S_{N}}}\Phi(s,x)I^{N,\pm}_{i}(x,u_{N},A,\xi^{N}_{0})

(7.28)

and

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{N,2}_{i}(\xi^{N}_{0},u_{N},\Phi_{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{N}}r^{N,a}(\sqrt{N}A)\widehat{E}\Big(h_{i,2}^{N,+}(\xi^{N}_{0},u_{N},A,\Phi_{s})\Big)

(7.29)
